signing key on a pair the registry does not serve for signing: `ArgumentException`).

- **An assertion issued beyond the skew is not yet valid; one reader serves the RFC 7523 claim rules, `PrivateKeyJwtClientAuthentication`, `FederationClientAuthentication`, `JwsAccessTokenValidator` and `Oidc10IdTokenValidator`'s `auth_time`.** [RFC 7523 Section 3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7523#section-3): "In order to issue an access token response as described in OAuth 2.0 [RFC6749] or to rely on a JWT for client authentication, the authorization server MUST validate the JWT according to the criteria below. Application of additional restrictions and policy are at the discretion of the authorization server." [RFC 7519 Section 4.1.6](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7519#section-4.1.6): "The "iat" (issued at) claim identifies the time at which the JWT was issued. This claim can be used to determine the age of the JWT. Its value MUST be a number containing a NumericDate value. Use of this claim is OPTIONAL."
  - **The refusal.** An `iat` later than the current instant plus the skew is refused, this library's policy under RFC 7523 Section 3's second sentence: in the shared time rules (`Rfc7523AssertionValidation.ValidateTemporalClaims`) as `NotYetValid` with "The assertion iat is in the future beyond the skew tolerance.", so `Rfc7523AssertionValidation.Validate` answers `Rfc7523AssertionValidationFailureReason.NotYetValid` and `IdJagAssertionValidation` answers `IdJagValidationFailureReason.NotYetValid` through its map of the shared outcome; and in `FederationClientAuthentication.Validate` with "The client assertion iat is in the future beyond the skew tolerance.", the refusal and the words of `PrivateKeyJwtClientAuthentication`. An `iat` equal to the current instant plus the skew is accepted. The check follows the `exp`-at-or-before-`iat` check and compares the two instants through their difference, as the `nbf` check does. At the token endpoint the application's assertion delegate answers such an assertion with no grant, so the JWT bearer grant and the ID-JAG redeem answer 400 `{"error":"invalid_grant","error_description":"The JWT assertion is not valid."}` ([RFC 7523 Section 3.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7523#section-3.1): "The value of the "error" parameter MUST be the "invalid_grant" error code."), and a federation endpoint whose gate validates the client assertion answers `invalid_client`.
  - **No maximum age.** No rule refuses an `iat` far in the past ([RFC 7523 Section 3](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7523#section-3) item 6: "Note that the authorization server may reject JWTs with an "iat" claim value that is unreasonably far in the past."): the age of an assertion is the application's delegate's to judge, and no parameter carries one.
  - **One reader.** `Rfc7523AssertionValidation.ReadNumericDate` (internal) reads the NumericDates of five users: the RFC 7523 claim rules, `PrivateKeyJwtClientAuthentication`, `FederationClientAuthentication`, the `exp`, `iat` and `nbf` of `JwsAccessTokenValidator` and the `auth_time` of `Oidc10IdTokenValidator`. A number a decoder hands as a `long`, `int`, `short`, `byte`, `ulong`, `uint`, `double` or `decimal` is read with its fraction kept; a `float` and every other shape are malformed (a `float` is a number whose 24 mantissa bits hold only one epoch second in 128 of this era exactly: between 2^30 and 2^31 seconds its spacing is 128 seconds, so an instant read from one is off by up to 64 seconds; refusing the shape as a whole is this library's rule, and the RFC 7523 claim rules and the ID-JAG describe a `float` claim as a NumericDate this server cannot represent), and so are a JSON string and a number no instant can hold. The JAR, SIOP, JARM, OID4VCI proof and `BearerTokenValidation` validators read their NumericDates with their own readers (`src/Verifiable.OAuth/Jar/JwtClaimReaders.cs`, `src/Verifiable.OAuth/Siop/SelfIssuedIdTokenValidation.cs`, `src/Verifiable.OAuth/Jarm/JarmResponseValidation.cs`, `src/Verifiable.OAuth/Oid4Vci/CredentialProofValidator.cs`, `src/Verifiable.OAuth/Server/BearerTokenValidation.cs`).
  - **The ID-JAG's REQUIRED claims on the wire.** [draft-ietf-oauth-identity-assertion-authz-grant-04 Section 3.1](https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/draft-ietf-oauth-identity-assertion-authz-grant-04#section-3.1): "iss: REQUIRED - The issuer identifier of the IdP Authorization Server as defined in [RFC8414]." and "exp: REQUIRED - as defined in Section 4.1.4 of [RFC7519]."; a grant without either is refused at the redeem as `MissingIssuer` or `MissingExpiration`, as a grant without `jti` or `iat` is.
  - Proven by `Rfc7523AssertionValidationTests.TheEdgeOfTheSkewDecidesTheIssuedAtToTheTick` (an `iat` one skew after the clock accepted and surfaced; a tick later `NotYetValid` with the description), `IdJagAssertionValidationTests.TheEdgeOfTheSkewDecidesTheIssuedAtToTheTick` (the same through the ID-JAG map), `JwtBearerGrantTests.AnAssertionIssuedBeyondTheSkewIsRefusedAsNotValidAsync` (over the wire, `iat` the clock plus the skew plus one second: 400 with the body above, the recorded `NotYetValid` and description), `JwtBearerGrantTests.AnAssertionIssuedAtTheEdgeOfTheSkewIsValidAsync` (over the wire, the edge: 200 and an access token), `IdJagGrantTests.AnIdJagIssuedBeyondTheSkewIsRefusedAtTheRedeemAsync` (over the wire, `iat` the clock plus 61 seconds: 400, recorded `NotYetValid`, the association not called), `IdJagGrantTests.AnIdJagIssuedAtTheEdgeOfTheSkewIsRedeemedAsync` (over the wire, plus 60 seconds: 200), `FederationFetchEndpointTests.FetchEndpointRefusesAClientAssertionIssuedBeyondTheSkew` (over the wire, the client assertion in a POST form body, plus 61 seconds: 400 `{"error":"invalid_client","error_description":"The client assertion iat is in the future beyond the skew tolerance."}`, the resolver not consulted), `FederationFetchEndpointTests.FetchEndpointServesAClientAssertionIssuedAtTheEdgeOfTheSkew` (over the wire, POST, plus 60 seconds: 200 and the Subordinate Statement verifying under the anchor's federation public key), `IdJagGrantTests.AnIdJagWithoutIssOrExpIsRefusedAtTheRedeemAsync` (over the wire: `MissingIssuer`, `MissingExpiration`), `Rfc7523AssertionValidationTests.AFractionalNumericDateKeepsItsFraction` (an `iat` and an `exp` a fraction apart within the clock's own second), `JwsAccessTokenValidatorTests.ValidatorRefusesATimeClaimThatIsNoRepresentableNumericDateAsMalformed` (an `exp`, `iat` or `nbf` that is a string of digits, an `iat` of 1e15: `Malformed`), `JwsAccessTokenValidatorTests.ValidatorReadsTheTimeClaimsInEachNumberShapeTheReaderNames` (the `exp`, `nbf` and `iat` handed in each of the eight shapes: valid, the instants to the tick), `JwsAccessTokenValidatorTests.ValidatorRefusesATimeClaimHandedAsAFloatAsMalformed` (an `exp`, `iat` or `nbf` handed as a `float`: `Malformed`), `Rfc7523AssertionValidationTests.ATimeClaimAsAFloatIsInconsistent` (an `exp`, `iat` or `nbf` as a `float`: `InconsistentTemporalClaims` with "The assertion &lt;claim&gt; claim is a NumericDate this server cannot represent."), `IdJagAssertionValidationTests.AnIssuedAtAsAFloatIsANumericDateThisServerCannotRepresent` (an `iat` handed as a `float`: `InconsistentTemporalClaims` with the same description through the ID-JAG map), `PrivateKeyJwtClientAuthenticationTests.AnIssuedAtAsAFloatIsRejectedAsMalformed` (an `iat` handed as a `float`: rejected, "The client assertion iat claim is malformed."), `FederationClientAuthenticationTests.AnIssuedAtAsAFloatIsRejectedAsMalformed` (an `iat` handed as a `float`: rejected, "The client authentication JWT iat claim is malformed."), `Oidc10IdTokenNonceAudienceScenarioTests.IdTokenAuthTimeIsReadInEachNumberShapeTheReaderNames` (over the wire, the `auth_time` handed in each of the eight shapes: surfaced to the tick), `Oidc10IdTokenNonceAudienceScenarioTests.IdTokenAuthTimeHandedAsAFloatIsRejected` (over the wire, an `auth_time` handed as a `float`: `Malformed`), and ---

## 9. Token producer selection & identity issuance

The token endpoint issues one JWT per applicable `TokenProducer` per request. Which producers run, and what identity claims they're allowed to carry, is decided across three independent layers rather than one central switch. See `documents/ADRs/token-producer-selection-and-openid-anchoring.md` for the decision record; this section describes the resulting shape.

### 9.1 Endpoint match gates the grant capability

Each of the six token-issuing grants — `authorization_code`, `refresh_token`, `client_credentials`, `token_exchange`, `jwt_bearer`, `pre_authorized_code` — is its own `EndpointCandidate` carrying a `Capability` (`WellKnownCapabilityIdentifiers.OAuthAuthorizationCode` / `OAuthClientCredentials` / `OAuthTokenExchange` / `OAuthJwtBearer` / `Oid4VciPreAuthorizedCodeGrant`). `EndpointChain.MatchAsync` (§2.2) only reaches a grant's `BuildInputAsync` when the registration's resolved capability set allows that capability, so by the time an `IssuanceContext` is constructed the request has already proven the tenant may run this grant at all — the coarsest gate in the pipeline runs once, before any producer is considered.

`IssuanceContext.GrantType` (the wire `grant_type`, from `WellKnownGrantTypes`) carries that grant identity forward into the producer walk. It is not a re-check of what the endpoint match already gated — it's an independent signal producers read for decisions the endpoint match cannot express, because the endpoint match only knows "this tenant may run this grant", not "this grant establishes an authenticated End-User" or any other per-producer semantic the grant identity implies.

### 9.2 Producer selection: optional feature gate + grant-aware `IsApplicable`

`TokenProducer.RequiredCapability` is `CapabilityIdentifier?` — a coarse tenant-*feature* gate, orthogonal to the grant capability the endpoint match already checked. `null` means "not feature-gated at all"; the producer's applicability then rests entirely on `IsApplicable`.

- `Rfc9068AccessTokenProducer` sets `RequiredCapability = null`. An RFC 9068 access token is the default response of every token-issuing grant, and the grant's own endpoint match already gated the request before the producer walk begins — there is nothing left for a second capability check to gate. Its `IsApplicable` is unconditionally `true`.
- `Oidc10IdTokenProducer` keeps `RequiredCapability = WellKnownCapabilityIdentifiers.OidcOpenIdConnect`. Whether a tenant offers ID Tokens at all is a genuine opt-in feature, independent of which grant capability the request matched — a tenant can be `client_credentials`-only and never have OIDC wired at all. Its `IsApplicable` (`IsApplicableAsync`) is `openid ∈ scope AND GrantType ∈ {authorization_code, refresh_token}` — grant-aware, and checked independently of the capability gate (see §9.4).

The walk itself — `IssueTokensAsync` in `AuthCodeEndpoints.cs` — applies both filters per producer, in order: skip when `RequiredCapability` is set and absent from `context.ResolvedCapabilities`; skip when `IsApplicable` returns `false`; otherwise resolve the signing key for the producer's `KeyUsage` via `SigningKeySelection.ResolveSigningKeyIdAsync`, call `BuildAsync`, merge claim-contributor output, and sign. A producer that needs a coarser tenant switch than its own `IsApplicable` logic can express reaches for `RequiredCapability`; a producer whose applicability depends on values already on `IssuanceContext` (scope, grant type, or anything else on the record) expresses it in `IsApplicable` instead. The two are deliberately different axes, not a fallback chain.

### 9.3 One shared issuer

`IssueTokensAsync` is the one producer-walk loop of the six token-issuing grant sites: it owns the `RequiredCapability`/`IsApplicable` filter, the signing material's resolution, the algorithm derivation (`CryptoFormatConversions.DefaultTagToJwaConverter` from the resolved material's `Tag`), `BuildAsync`, the claim-contributor merge, `UnsignedJwt.SignAsync` and the compact serialization, and it returns a `TokenIssuanceResult` (the compact JWS and an `IssuedTokenAudit` of `jti`, `keyid`, `iat` and `exp` per response field, plus the latest `exp` across every token issued in the walk) or a `server_error` `ServerHttpResponse`, either when a producer's signing material could not be resolved, or, with `WellKnownOAuthErrorDescriptions.ServerNotReady` and no token of the walk returned, when a payload's `sub` after the claim-contributor merge names no subject (a blank string for any producer, and for the ID Token and the access token a `sub` that is absent or is not a string; see the section 8 entry "A blank subject is no subject, at every seam that supplies one.").

Each grant site keeps only what is genuinely grant-specific: constructing its `IssuanceContext` before the call, and shaping the response after it. `authorization_code` and `refresh_token` fold the audit dict into PDA state for `BuildResponse`; `client_credentials`, `token_exchange`, `jwt_bearer`, and `pre_authorized_code` write a scalar `expires_in` (read from `IssuedAudits[WellKnownTokenTypes.AccessToken]` rather than tracked separately) plus inline JSON. There is exactly one signing path in the library; a producer added for a new token type is correct at all six sites the moment it is added to `AuthorizationServerIntegration.TokenProducers`.

### 9.4 The `openid` ⇒ end-user invariant

`openid` in a token's scope is supposed to mean "this token identifies an authenticated End-User" — every ID Token and every UserInfo response makes that promise. The library defines its **end-user-authenticating grants** as `{authorization_code, refresh_token}`: the two grants where the library itself established an End-User authentication (refresh continues a prior one). `client_credentials` authenticates a client, not a person — its `sub` is the `client_id`. `pre_authorized_code` establishes no session at all. `token_exchange` and `jwt_bearer` are neither — whether the exchanged or asserted subject is an End-User is a decision the app's own authorization seam makes, not something the library can infer from the grant shape alone.

The invariant is enforced at three independent layers so that a defect in any one of them cannot by itself mint an identity assertion for a non-End-User subject:

1. **Source — scope narrowing.** `DropIdentityScopesForNonEndUserGrant` (`AuthCodeEndpoints.cs`) strips `openid` and the OIDC Core §5.4 identity scopes (`profile`/`email`/`address`/`phone`) from the granted scope before it reaches `IssuanceContext.Scope`, per the RFC 6749 §3.3 narrowing allowance. It runs at exactly the two grant sites with no authenticated End-User by construction — `client_credentials` and `pre_authorized_code` — and emits `OAuthEventNames.IdentityScopesDroppedForNonEndUserGrant` (an `ActivityEvent` tagged with the dropped scopes) when narrowing actually occurs, so a deployment can observe the drop rather than have it happen silently. `token_exchange` and `jwt_bearer` do **not** call it: their authorization seams (`AuthorizationServerIntegration.AuthorizeTokenExchangeAsync`, `ValidateJwtBearerAssertionAsync`) own whether the exchanged/asserted subject is an End-User, and the app opts into identity claims by granting `openid` itself.
2. **Consumer — the ID Token producer.** `Oidc10IdTokenProducer.IsApplicableAsync`'s grant-type check (§9.2) is independent of the scope check — it reads `IssuanceContext.GrantType` directly. Even if a defect ever left `openid` on a `client_credentials` or `token_exchange` token's scope, this producer still declines to synthesize an ID Token unless the grant is one the library itself recognizes as end-user-authenticating.
3. **Consumer — UserInfo.** `UserInfoEndpoints` keeps its pre-existing `openid`-in-token-scope check (OIDC Core §5.3.1), and that check is correct *because* layer 1 guarantees `client_credentials` and `pre_authorized_code` tokens never carry `openid`. A `token_exchange` or `jwt_bearer` token that legitimately carries `openid` is correctly served — the app vouched for the subject by granting it. Absent the source-side guarantee, this check alone would be exactly the failure mode `WellKnownScopes` warns against in its own documentation: an access token issued without `openid` authorizes actions but does not identify a user, and its presence must never substitute for proof of identity.

No single layer is trusted alone. The source layer is the one that actually prevents the leak; the two consumer layers are defense-in-depth against a defect that reaches them anyway.
